- **Summary**: Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.



### PlayCraft: Designing Engaging Games from Agent Play Experience
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26624v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26624v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 18:59:34+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 18:59:34+00:00
- **Authors**: Weihao Cheng
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent AI agents can generate playable games, but these games are not necessarily engaging. Mastery is a key source of engagement, yet designing for it requires extensive human play and tuning. We propose PlayCraft, an automated framework that uses a vision-based agent to play games and draws on its detailed play experience to revise them for mastery. Starting from an initial game, PlayCraft alternates between playing and crafting. In the playing stage, a player agent watches the game screen, uses the controls, and pursues the game objective over repeated episodes while retaining memory. After each episode, it reports its progress, outcomes, decisions, and reflections. In the crafting stage, a crafter agent uses these reports to revise the game. It estimates player abilities, predicts performance at different proficiency levels, and compares those predictions with a target learning progression. It diagnoses the game and addresses the gaps by giving players meaningful opportunities to improve through better decisions and execution. We evaluate games in fresh runs with player agents across three intelligence settings, measuring learning gain and learning slope. Compared with the initial games, PlayCraft yields larger learning gains and steeper learning slopes in most games. It also outperforms the baselines on the macro-average of each metric at every intelligence setting.



### RL Post-Training in Diffusion Transformers Is Activation Steering
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26627v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26627v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 18:59:53+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 18:59:53+00:00
- **Authors**: Tuna Han Salih Meral, Kaan Oktay, Alec Helbling, Atakan Kara, Duen Horng Chau, Pinar Yanardag
- **Comment**: Project page: https://rl-is-steering.github.io
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training substantially improves the visual quality, text rendering, and compositional accuracy of diffusion transformers. Because capabilities such as rendering requested text or composing multiple objects are inherently prompt-dependent, a natural hypothesis is that improving them requires learning complex, input-dependent transformations. To isolate how much of these gains an input-independent shift explains, we probe each RL-tuned model with a deliberately simple parameterization: a fixed set of steering vectors added to the block outputs of the frozen base model. On SD3.5-M, these vectors hold 65x fewer parameters than the RL-tuned LoRA (0.29M against 18.8M). We fit them to match the velocity predictions of public RL-tuned checkpoints, with no reward in the objective. Although the vectors are identical for every prompt, token, and sampling seed, they recover 90.0-96.3% of the reward gains of six RL-tuned image models, and 89.8% and 111.9% on two RL-tuned video models. Initialized at zero and trained with online RL, the vectors alone exceed the reward gains of weight-based RL on text rendering (OCR) and human preference (PickScore) on SD3.5-M. A constant shift thus suffices for most of the gain of policy-gradient RL post-training: without new input-dependent transformations, it steers the pretrained generator toward what it can already compute.



### Posterior Matching Distillation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26629v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26629v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 18:59:57+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 18:59:57+00:00
- **Authors**: Hidir Yesiltepe, Julius Berner, Arash Vahdat, Pinar Yanardag
- **Comment**: Project Page: https://pmd-distillation.github.io/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Diffusion distillation accelerates image and video generation by compressing a pretrained teacher's iterative sampling process into a few network evaluations. Distribution-based methods achieve this by matching the student's output distribution to the teacher's. This matching is often driven by reverse-KL objectives, whose mode-seeking bias favors dominant teacher modes at the expense of diversity across samples. We introduce Posterior Matching Distillation (PMD), a new member of the distribution-based distillation family that combines mode-covering posterior sampling with any-step generation. At zero population loss, PMD recovers the teacher's full posterior of clean images at each trained noise level. Its stochastic student supports any-step sampling budgets within the trained timestep grid without further training. To learn these posteriors, PMD matches exponentially reweighted averages of student samples conditioned on a fixed noisy state to corresponding teacher posterior means. Under Gaussian corruption, exponentially tilted posterior mean is a single teacher evaluation at a shifted input. We prove that, matching tilted means over an open neighborhood uniquely identifies the full posterior. PMD thus supervises the entire conditional distribution through a family of mean constraints, without any auxiliary student-score network. Experiments on SDXL and FLUX.2 [klein] 4B demonstrate that PMD achieves the best FID among 4-step methods on both models, while maintaining competitive or leading performance across prompt-alignment and perceptual-quality metrics.



### Model the Annotation, Not the Noise: Semantic Annotation-Channel Prompt Learning against Noisy Labels
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26702v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26702v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 19:01:39+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 19:01:39+00:00
- **Authors**: Zibin Geng, Yitong Bao, Jia Li, Xuefeng Jiang, Sheng Sun, Yuwei Wang, Min Liu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Parameter-efficient prompt learning has become a cornerstone for adapting pre-trained vision-language models (VLMs), yet it remains highly vulnerable to label noise in few-shot Web and retrieval scenarios. Existing noise robust methods typically rely on hard sample selection or deterministic label correction, which discard valuable semantic ambiguity and overlook how observed annotations are actually generated. To bridge this gap, we reformulate noisy prompt learning through the perspective of data generation, proposing SACP, a Semantic-aware Annotation Channel-based Prompt learning framework. Instead of suppressing noise heuristically, SACP decouples latent clean-label prediction from instance-dependent annotation generation. By integrating frozen VLM semantic priors with lightweight visual residuals, SACP constructs a structured annotation channel optimized via generalized expectation-maximization with soft posteriors. Furthermore, a posterior-aware memory is introduced to preserve label uncertainty during test-time inference. Extensive experiments across different synthetic benchmarks and the real-world noisy Food101N dataset demonstrate that SACP achieves state-of-the-art performance against its counterparts in different label noise patterns, especially under severe asymmetric noise.



### MambaSplit: Severity-Robust and Inference-Efficient Semantic Unmixing of Fluorescence Microscopy Image Data
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26703v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **eess.IV**, cs.CV, q-bio.QM
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26703v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 19:02:02+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 19:02:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Aman Kukde, Ruggiero Santeramo, Federico Carrara, Ashesh Ashesh, Igor Zubarev, Florian Jug
- **Comment**: 28 pages, 15 figures, 23 tables; main paper (8 pages) followed by supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: The number of structures fluorescence microscopy can image in one sample is limited by how few fluorophores can be separated without significant spectral overlap. Semantic unmixing circumvents this limit by imaging two structures in one fluorescent channel and computationally separating the superimposed image into one image per structure. Because pixels rarely identify their structure, unmixing requires both local and long-range spatial context. The relative brightness of the two structures is unknown and can be very uneven, so predictions must remain accurate across mixing severities, from balanced mixtures to ones where a structure is barely visible. Existing methods either process small image patches or add context through auxiliary pathways that slow inference on large images. We present MambaSplit, a hierarchical state-space encoder with a convolutional decoder that captures context across large tiles in linear time and predicts both channels in one deterministic pass. MambaSplit trains either on computationally summed channel images with random mixing ratios, where a lightweight head estimates the unknown ratio and conditions the decoder on it, or on experimentally acquired superpositions. Across five datasets and nine mixing ratios, MambaSplit achieves the highest RI-PSNR, by up to 3.0 dB, in 11 of 15 dataset-severity combinations and ties in one more, while running 5.9x faster than the strongest baseline. On acquired superpositions, fine-tuning a model pretrained on summed inputs matches or exceeds all baselines. Ablations show that accuracy and robustness come mainly from the training recipe: large tiles, mixing ratios sampled across their full range (up to 11 dB over fixed-ratio training), and independently drawn channel crops (3.9 dB on BioSR). The state-space backbone makes large tiles affordable, enabling high-content screening and event-driven smart microscopy.



### Forehead ROI Localization under Real-World Imaging Degradations via Cross-View Pseudo-Labeling
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26712v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26712v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 19:13:39+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 19:13:39+00:00
- **Authors**: Geetanjali Sharma, Gaurav Jaswal, Aditya Nigam, Raghavendra Ramachandra
- **Comment**: 9 pages, submitted and accepted to the 2027 IEEE/CVF Winter Conference on Applications of Computer Vision Workshops (WACVW)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Forehead region-of-interest (ROI) localization is important for forehead-creases biometrics, particularly when the lower face is occluded. However, localization remains challenging under varying image quality, pose, and illumination. We propose Confidence and Consistency Guided Pseudo-Labeling (CCGP), which adapts Grounding DINO using unlabeled facial images filtered by detection confidence and geometric consistency between original and horizontally flipped views, without modifying the detector architecture or loss. We evaluate CCGP on five external datasets and 350 images spanning seven imaging conditions. CCGP achieves the highest mean intersection over union (mIoU) on four of five external datasets, improving from 0.415 to 0.580 on RMFRD compared with supervised Grounding DINO. On the quality-stratified subset, the largest gains occur under blur (0.487 to 0.719) and low contrast (0.615 to 0.710). CCGP also outperforms conventional self-training on RMFRD (0.580 versus 0.551 mIoU). Despite a slight in-domain performance gap (0.861 vs. 0.866 mIoU) and challenges with off-angle poses, CCGP improves cross-dataset localization under challenging imaging conditions.



### SCATR-ITS: a scalable transformer for large-area individual tree segmentation from LiDAR point clouds
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26715v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26715v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 19:14:58+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 19:14:58+00:00
- **Authors**: Lassi Ruoppa, Verneri Seppänen, Antero Kukko, Juha Hyyppä
- **Comment**: 38 pages, 13 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Individual tree segmentation (ITS) from LiDAR point clouds is one of the most widely studied segmentation tasks in forestry. Although deep learning methods define the state of the art on the majority of ITS benchmarks, their high computational complexity and long runtime currently prevent their adoption in large-scale operational workflows. In this work, we present SCATR-ITS, a scalable transformer model specifically designed for efficient ITS over large geographical areas. Based on the ForestFormer3D framework, SCATR-ITS transitions to superpoint-level processing to accelerate inference, with the superpoints constructed from learned embeddings. The architecture incorporates size-aware superpoint-level postprocessing, a dynamic query count module to handle high variation in forest structure, and a maximal input stride designed to minimize prediction redundancy. SCATR-ITS achieves accuracy on par with the state of the art on the FGI-EMIT and FOR-InstanceV2 datasets with an up to 8.5$\times$ higher inference speed. In addition, SCATR-ITS has a significantly lower GPU VRAM footprint during both training and inference, and exhibits excellent zero-shot generalization across multiple datasets. To demonstrate large-area ITS capabilities, SCATR-ITS is applied to a 142 km$^2$ region of Finnish national laser scanning data where it achieves an average throughput of 13.8 min/km$^2$, $\sim$18.6$\times$ faster than ForestFormer3D and approaching the speed of the unsupervised watershed algorithm, which it vastly outperforms in accuracy and segment quality. Overall, SCATR-ITS delivers state-of-the-art accuracy at speeds where operational-scale adoption becomes viable, paving the way toward countrywide deployment. The source code for SCATR-ITS and pretrained weights are available at https://github.com/ruoppa/scatr_its.



### Decomposing images in a PRISM: A Single-Source Multi-View Framework for Image Representation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26726v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26726v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 19:23:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 19:23:01+00:00
- **Authors**: Eduardo Campos, Willam Cardoso, Diego Furtado, Solange Oliveira, Alneu Lopes
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multi-view learning has shown that combining complementary views can improve predictive performance, yet many datasets provide a single raw source of information. How can multiple useful views be constructed from one source? We propose PRISM (Plural Representations of Images via Structural Maps), a framework that transforms an image into representations with distinct inductive biases through explicit structural maps, together with a protocol for measuring what each view contributes. We evaluate PRISM-HD, a hand-designed instantiation of PRISM in which the constructed views are summarized through representation-specific descriptors, on three artistic and four non-artistic image datasets. Analyses at a fixed feature budget show that the gains of the structural views are not explained by feature dimensionality alone. PRISM-HD by itself outperforms WND-CHARM on all seven datasets. Moreover, PRISM-HD and Fisher Vector provide complementary predictive signals: their equal-weight combination improves over the better individual representation by 5--8 percentage points on the artistic datasets. When combined with fine-tuned deep networks through a fixed parameter-free fusion rule, the PRISM-based representation improves performance on four of seven datasets and leaves it statistically unchanged on the remaining three, indicating that the constructed views provide predictive signals that are complementary to learned deep representations.



### SparseHPS: A Unified Model for Human Pose and Shape across Depth Cameras and LiDARs
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26730v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26730v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 19:25:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 19:25:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Ilya Zaychuk
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 3 figures, 6 tables. Project page: https://ghostlate.github.io/SparseHPS
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recovering 3D human pose and shape from point clouds is central to motion capture, robotics and self-driving vehicles, because depth cameras and LiDARs sense a person directly in metric 3D and work under any lighting. However, current approaches are each tailored to one sensor type: point density differs by more than ten times between devices, public datasets are labeled with incompatible body models, and LiDAR approaches need long input sequences to offset sparse scans. In this work, we present SparseHPS, a novel sensor-agnostic network that recovers SMPL-X pose, shape and global translation from a human point cloud with one set of weights, for single frames and streams alike. Specifically, SparseHPS encodes the cloud with a LitePT backbone over sparse voxels, represents every joint by a learnable query decoded with one common head, localizes the body by voting over voxels, and draws temporal context from a causal memory of tokens from the seven preceding frames. A single model is trained on ten datasets that mix rendered clouds with real depth-camera and LiDAR captures, all converted to one body model. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art results on the SLOPER4D and FreeMotion LiDAR benchmarks, where SparseHPS lowers the root-relative joint error by 22% and 11%, respectively, compared with the strongest prior method, which needs 32 frames. On real Azure Kinect captures of held-out HuMMan subjects, SparseHPS attains a joint error of 34.7 mm. Moreover, the current frame alone already yields the bulk of this accuracy.



### INCISE: Training-Free In-Context Segmentation of Surgical Videos
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26734v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26734v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 19:29:15+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 19:29:15+00:00
- **Authors**: Yifan Xu, Bofang Zheng, Guangyu Meng, Nishchal Sapkota, Xianshi Ma, Xu Cao, Tianjiao Yu, Danny Z Chen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Surgical video analysis requires video object segmentation (VOS), yet existing methods are commonly based on pretrained model fine-tuning using a large number of expert labels. In-context segmentation (ICS) offers the promise of bypassing this high cost by segmenting a target video using a few annotated references, which are the sole source of surgical knowledge for a frozen model. However, existing ICS methods tend to draw such references at random, which concentrate on a few views that dominate a surgical video and can miss other objects of interest (e.g., transient instruments and small structures). To address these challenges, we propose INCISE, a training-free ICS framework for surgical videos. INCISE selects unlabeled frames from multiple surgical videos that jointly cover the local appearances of a target video, and then segments the target video using these references. With only 1.7% of training frames selected and annotated as references and no target video frame labeled, INCISE obtains 67.2% mean mIoU and outperforms the best SOTA fine-tuned method by 9.2 points. We hope INCISE can help establish ICS as a practical alternative to fine-tuning in surgical video analysis and other domains with a costly labeling burden.



### ConRef: Auditing Reference Reliability in 2D/3D Registration Benchmarks
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26758v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26758v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 19:54:20+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 19:54:20+00:00
- **Authors**: Jianfeng Yue, Feng Li, Tianyu Song, Julius Watrinet, Ulrich Eck, Nassir Navab
- **Comment**: 12 pages, 6 figures, 1 table; includes 4 pages of supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Purpose: Accurate 2D/3D registration is crucial for image-guided orthopedic interventions, enabling the alignment of preoperative 3D anatomy with intraoperative X-ray observations for surgical navigation. Public benchmarks have facilitated the development and evaluation of increasingly accurate registration methods. However, as registration errors approach the uncertainty of benchmark reference poses, measured performance may be limited by reference inaccuracies rather than algorithmic errors alone. Methods: We propose Consensus Reference (ConRef), a multi-view framework for auditing reference poses in existing X-ray/computed tomography (CT) registration benchmarks. By jointly exploiting multiple X-ray observations, ConRef estimates an additional reference pose, quantifies its uncertainty, and identifies potential X-ray outliers. We validate the framework using independent anatomical evidence and a newly acquired X-ray/cone-beam CT (CBCT) phantom dataset with bead-based reference poses. Results: On our BeadPhant dataset, ConRef differed from independent bead-based references by 0.08-0.12 mm, while discrepancies with original references reached 2.06 mm on the Femur dataset. It also flagged 22 DeepFluoro X-rays as potential outliers. On Femur F5, reference choice changed the submillimeter success conclusions of the same registrations. Conclusion: Our findings demonstrate that reference discrepancies can influence the evaluation of high-accuracy 2D/3D registration methods. By providing an additional, independently validated reference and identifying potential inconsistencies, ConRef enables systematic auditing of existing benchmarks and supports more reliable evaluation of registration error.



### TacHOI: Contact-Aware Object Reconstruction and Tracking for Egocentric Videos
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26764v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26764v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 20:01:10+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 20:01:10+00:00
- **Authors**: Jackson Codd, Haoyu Li, Xianyi Cheng
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Egocentric videos offer a scalable source of human demonstrations for robot learning. However, recovering metric object geometry and motion from monocular video is hindered by scale-depth ambiguity and hand-induced occlusion, while image-derived contact and shape priors alone cannot uniquely determine metric scale. We introduce TacHOI, a training-free framework that integrates tactile contact with monocular vision for joint hand-object reconstruction and tracking, without requiring object templates or depth sensors. Starting from pretrained visual estimates, TacHOI uses tactile contact to constrain object depth relative to the estimated hand, while spatially separated contacts on a metrically calibrated hand provide a physical ruler for object extent. It incorporates these constraints into a joint optimization of hand poses, object trajectories, and anisotropic object scale alongside visual alignment. Across 100 ARCTIC and HO3D sequences with synthesized tactile observations, TacHOI achieves the lowest median ADD-S* among the evaluated baselines, reducing error from 15.6 to 3.6 cm on ARCTIC and achieving 2.8 cm on HO3D. Additional experiments on OpenTouch demonstrate its applicability to measured tactile observations.



### FACET: A Data-Efficient Regional Parametric Head Model
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26773v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.GR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26773v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 20:08:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 20:08:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Mathieu Lamarre, Elly Akhoundi, Thibault Goyallon, Patrick Anderson, Étienne Danvoye
- **Comment**: 4 pages, 5 figures, 1 table. Accepted to SIGGRAPH Asia 2026 Technical Communications (SA Technical Communications '26)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Production face pipelines need parametric head models that are expressive, FACS-controllable, and buildable on a small, fixed pool of identities. More training identities would always help, but high-quality registered head scans are expensive to capture; when scaling the data is not an option, the remaining lever is architecture. We present FACET, a parametric head model that makes the most of a fixed identity pool through architectural locality rather than more scans, and makes three contributions, each isolated against a matched global-PCA ablation on our own topology, training set, and normalization. (1) A regional identity decomposition into 16 anatomical regions bypasses the N-1 rank ceiling of a global PCA, driving held-out neutral-fit error from a 1.95 mm floor down to 0.23 mm on the same training identities. (2) A learned FACS action-unit expression model with per-identity neural personalization not only stays FACS-controllable but beats a properly-regularized global expression PCA at every component count -- refuting the argument that FACS is too overcomplete to fit. (3) A dedicated neck/shoulder region reduces pose leakage into the pose-invariant upper face during unposing. Together, these show that architectural locality is the effective lever for a production head model under a fixed identity budget.



### What Helps Language-Model Agents Review Violent Video? A Controlled Comparison with a Human Evidence Reference
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26776v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CL**, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.MM
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26776v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 20:10:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 20:10:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Mohammed El Amine Mokhtari, Matei Mancas, Virginie Vandenbulcke
- **Comment**: 17 pages, 1 figure, 12 tables. Code: https://github.com/amine0110/evidence-graph-agents ; project page: https://amine0110.github.io/evidence-graph-agents/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Systems that flag violent video for a human operator can now be built from language models without training, by combining perception models and letting a language model decide. We ask how the observations of the perception models should reach the decision, and which evidence matters. On the XD-Violence test set (799 clips) we fix one set of perception agents (video description, object detection, sound tagging, speech recognition and speech tagging) and one reasoning model, and compare three interfaces: messages between agents, a typed evidence graph explored with tools, and flat text read in one pass; and a single audio-visual model given the raw clip. Two annotators provide a human reference that records the decision, the incident segments and whether they relied on sight, sound or speech. Flat text matches the single model (F1 0.967 against 0.968), while the graph agent and message passing are less accurate; a variant of the graph agent developed on a separate set did not close the gap, and both graph summaries and multi-turn tool use lowered accuracy. These results hold with reworded prompts and with a larger reasoning model, except that the larger model weakens message passing enough for the graph agent to beat it. Sound events improve F1 by +0.012 [+0.004, +0.021], in line with the annotators, who used sound or speech on most violent clips. Citations of stored observations are faithful, changing about half of the decisions when removed, but no more so with the graph than with flat text, and the agent pipeline costs about 3 times the GPU time of the single model. We also report two artefacts of vision-language describers that mislead pipelines built on them.



### Tracktail: Multi-View 3D Point Tracking without Depth Input
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26779v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26779v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 20:13:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 20:13:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Katie L Rupp, Jonathan Wong, Sharmishtaa Seshamani, Ana Mafalda Vicente, Lili Karashchuk
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We introduce Tracktail, a novel architecture designed to efficiently track query points in 3D from multiple calibrated views without any depth input. Our architecture separates the task as a per-camera video encoding of features and a per-query decoder. Notably, camera cross-attention is only performed across queries in the decoder, allowing for efficient inference (7 FPS and less than 4 GB memory for 10 views and 100 query points). We further introduce a per-camera scene scale computed from camera geometry, which allows us to normalize inputs and predictions appropriately across a vast variety of dataset scales. We train on a synthetic Multi-View Kubric dataset as well as a novel Multi-View Point Odyssey benchmark dataset, which consists of synthetic renders of humans and animals across 499 frames. Tracktail achieves state-of-the-art results on the Panoptic Studio, DexYCB, and Multi-View Kubric benchmarks, outperforming methods that rely on estimated depth. Tracktail also demonstrates a strong ability to maintain accurate tracking over longer ranges compared to existing methods. Our code, data, and weights are publicly available: https://ai-hhmi.github.io/tracktail/



### CADENCE:Temporally Coherent Whole-Body Human Mesh Recovery that Preserves Expressive Hand Motion
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26783v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.GR, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26783v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 20:20:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 20:20:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Keniel Peart, Jagmohan Chauhan, Indu P. Bodala, Shelly Vishwakarma
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Whole-body SMPL-X estimators recover body, hand, and face meshes from single images, but applied frame by frame to video they produce jittery motion. Smoothing the output removes the jitter at a cost that body-only benchmarks do not measure: a temporal filter strong enough to steady the torso also flattens the fast finger strokes that carry meaning in sign language and gesture. We present CADENCE, a whole-body SMPL-X system for monocular video designed around this trade-off. A per-frame estimator built on a shared C-RADIOv4 encoder, with a high-resolution hand pathway that reuses the same encoder on hand crops, produces each frame's estimate. An offline temporal refiner then filters the SMPL-X rotation sequence: attention along the kinematic tree selects, for every joint and every frame, a mixture of learned zero-phase filters, so the torso is smoothed while fast finger strokes pass through. On 3DPW, the refiner reduces acceleration error from 17.4 to 5.4 mm/s2 while lowering PA-MPJPE from 41.9 to 40.2 mm; generic smoothers applied to the same predictions reach 6.2 to 7.7 mm/s2. On SignAvatars/How2Sign sign-language sequences, the refiner has the lowest hand jitter among the compared smoothers while keeping a hand-speed peak amplitude of 0.98, against 0.35 for a SmoothNet retrained on the full SMPL-X vector. The per-frame estimator also obtains the lowest full-body mesh error (25.7 mm) and hand joint error (8.3 mm) among the compared methods on EHF.



### Spatial Query Execution as Verifiable Rewards for Multi-step Spatial Reasoning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26787v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26787v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 20:24:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 20:24:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Jiyoon Pyo, Dongwon Jung, Zekun Li, Nayeon Kim, Muhao Chen, Yao-Yi Chiang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) remain unreliable on spatial reasoning problems that require composing grounded relations across multiple objects. Yet, scaling reasoning supervision is challenging since human-annotated visual datasets often rely on fixed relational annotations and coarse object localization, making it difficult to obtain precise intermediate reasoning states across diverse spatial configurations and complexities. We introduce FARON (Fully Automated Reasoning Over spatial relatioNs), a framework that executes spatial queries to provide both execution-grounded reasoning supervision and verifiable outcome rewards. Rather than relying on existing annotated data, FARON constructs synthetic 2D and 3D scenes with explicit geometries, enabling configurable object arrangements, spatial relations, and multi-step compositions. Deterministic query execution yields intermediate sets and final answers, providing precise supervision without manual relation annotation. Our pipeline converts intermediate states into natural-language reasoning traces for supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while the execution-derived answers provide verifiable rewards for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Controlled experiments show that trace-based SFT improves multi-step spatial reasoning over final-answer supervision and that subsequent group relative policy optimization (GRPO) provides further gains. The resulting models also demonstrate transfer across external spatial reasoning benchmarks and multiple LVLM backbones without benchmark-specific fine-tuning. These results show the potential of using spatial query execution as a unified, flexible source of reasoning supervision and verifiable outcome rewards for multimodal spatial reasoning.



### Memorization and Generalization in Generative Models: A Perspective From Energy-based Modeling and Associative Memory
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26797v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV, cs.LG, stat.ML
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26797v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 20:40:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 20:40:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Bao Pham
- **Comment**: A thesis that discusses the coexistence of memorization and generalization in generative models
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Generative models now write working code, produce realistic images, and hold long conversations, yet the mechanisms behind their intelligence still evade our understanding. This thesis explores that puzzle through Associative Memory (AM), in which patterns are stored as energy minima and retrieved through attractor dynamics. Beyond their capacity, AMs form new minima known as spurious states, long regarded as a failure of memory. We argue that this failure is where creativity begins, and that memorization and generalization can coexist as outcomes of one retrieval process. First, we show that diffusion models behave as AMs. With little data, a diffusion model forms a distinct basin of attraction around each training sample. As the data grows, these basins shrink, and spurious states emerge as attractors that blend stored samples yet match none of them. They are the first signs of generative capability. We characterize this transition by measuring the volume, energy, and curvature of these basins across datasets and architectures, including Stable Diffusion. We then turn to language and move beyond the energy-based view of AM. We show that uniform-state discrete diffusion models are AMs capable of retrieving unseen data, with basins of attraction that arise from conditional likelihood maximization alone. As the training set grows, basins around training examples contract while basins around unseen examples expand, until corrupted test sentences are recovered as reliably as training ones. Conditional entropy exposes this coexistence within a single sequence and cheaply diagnoses where a model lies along the transition. Together, these results suggest that AM is a general principle defined by basins of attraction, and that memorization and generalization can coexist within one model. AM thus offers a framework for asking when a generative model recalls and when it creates.



### 3D Open-Vocabulary Detection via Visual Alignment and Generative Captions
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26842v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26842v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 21:53:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 21:53:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Thomas E. Huang, Alex Zihao Zhu, Hao Xiang, Tianyi Cai, Jingwei Ji, Yuliang Zou, Jiafan Yu, Xin Huang, Mingxing Tan
- **Comment**: Accepted to IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS) 2026
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Open-vocabulary 3D object detection remains a significant challenge for outdoor scenes, with existing methods often struggling to effectively transfer the rich semantic knowledge from powerful 2D Vision-Language Models (VLMs) into a 3D context. We propose a novel, two-part framework for this task. First, we introduce a direct, object-level distillation method. We generate class-agnostic 3D proposals to source regions from the entire scene, without relying on any manual labels. We leverage a spatially-aware VLM to extract a semantic embedding from each proposal's projected 2D bounding box and supervise our 3D model to predict this embedding on a per-instance basis. Second, to enhance zero-shot capability and inject domain-specific information, we employ a powerful Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) as an image captioner to generate rich textual captions for each of these proposals. These captions are integrated into the training objective to directly align the 3D features with these rich semantics, creating a composite loss that combines direct feature distillation with caption-based semantic enrichment. Our experiments validate the effectiveness of our approach, establishing a new state of the art in 3D open-vocabulary detection on the Waymo Open Dataset and nuScenes.



### Scoliosis5K: Multimodal Gait Analysis via Clip-Conditioned RGB-Silhouette Fusion
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26849v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26849v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 22:09:48+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 22:09:48+00:00
- **Authors**: Zizhao Peng, Zihan Wang, Zheng Lv, Yan Wang, Ping Li, Fengwei An
- **Comment**: 11 pages (8-page main paper and 3-page supplementary material), 3 figures, 12 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Color walking video records body boundary motion alongside clothing folds, surface appearance, and recording-environment cues. To examine what an explicit binary silhouette contributes to participant-level classification of scoliosis-related gait when a classifier already observes the paired color sequence, we construct Scoliosis5K, a multi-site collection of 4,998 corridor-walking sequences across 3,010 participant identifiers in which RGB person crops, binary silhouettes, and two-dimensional poses share each recording identifier and frame index. Our architecture uses each patch's pixel deviation from its clip mean to weight two low-rank query and value updates inside a pretrained RGB encoder, fusing the resulting video representation with a silhouette transformer sequence and regional kernel ridge scores extracted from one native-resolution RGB frame per window. Across 988 participants in five participant-disjoint cross-validation folds and three training seeds, the complete system reaches 83.77% accuracy and 79.91% macro-averaged F1, compared with 83.27% accuracy and 79.56% macro-F1 when the video RGB encoder remains frozen under identical inputs. Zeroing the silhouette vector at fixed weights shifts overall accuracy by only 0.10 percentage points while raising positive-category recall from 68.52% to 77.78% and lowering recall for both remaining categories. Macro-averaged AUROC is 0.950 across the cohort and 0.879 among participant pairs matched on acquisition site and recording-count group.



### FlowScope: 3D Scene Flow Pretraining Improves Surgical Robot Policies
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26865v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26865v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 22:31:27+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 22:31:27+00:00
- **Authors**: John J. Han, Adam Schmidt, Jingpei Lu, Zooey He, Muhammad Abdullah Jamal, Jie Ying Wu, Omid Mohareri
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Imitation learning for surgical robots is limited by the cost of collecting demonstrations. Meanwhile, many robot-assisted procedures are recorded as stereo video, yet policies cannot learn from them directly; clinical video carries no action labels. We present FlowScope, which pretrains a surgical policy network on unlabeled clinical video by forecasting scene flow, the metric 3D motion of scene points, with labels computed by off-the-shelf stereo depth and optical flow models. We pretrain the trunk of an Action Chunking Transformer (ACT) on a dataset of 1,149 clinical procedures (1.35M clips) and discard the flow head afterward, so the deployed policy is a standard ACT with no added inference cost. On a real surgical robotic system, FlowScope raises needle pickup success from 54% to 90% and first-handover success on needle regrasp from 4% to 52% (full task: 2% to 14%) over training from scratch (n=50 trials each), and retains its gains with a generative flow-matching action head. Ablations indicate that metric 3D motion is the strongest pretraining signal among those tested; kinematics, 2D tracks, depth alone, and scale-free 3D flow, each paired with kinematics, yield smaller or no gains. Representation analyses suggest that scene-flow pretraining encodes metric needle depth more accurately, that kinematics pretraining increases reliance on the robot state, and that needle-depth probes follow the ordering of real-robot success where offline validation error does not.



### SonoAtlasWorld: Anatomy-Grounded Ultrasound World Models for Wide-Range Scanning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26867v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26867v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 22:31:39+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 22:31:39+00:00
- **Authors**: Zheyuan Zhang, Boris Mailhe, Hang Yu, Yikang Liu, Xiao Chen, Ankush Mukherjee, Terrence Chen, Shanhui Sun
- **Comment**: Interactive video demonstrations are available at: https://sonoatlasworld.github.io/SonoAtlasWorld
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Learning robotic ultrasound policies requires interactive environments for scalable data generation and repeatable evaluation, while exploration on real patients is limited by acquisition costs and safety constraints. Generative world models offer an alternative, but image history and probe actions alone do not ensure anatomical consistency across wide-range scans and changing acoustic windows. We introduce SonoAtlasWorld, an ultrasound world model built on anatomy as state. A persistent 3D anatomical asset extracted from computed tomography (CT) anchors each scan to a specific anatomy. Probe actions determine anatomical and acoustic visibility conditions for a causal ultrasound generator trained on clinical videos. Our three-stage pipeline combines anatomy-conditioned pretraining, causal conversion and long-rollout alignment. Inference optimizations achieve pose-to-ultrasound throughput of 81.2 FPS. To evaluate anatomical control beyond image similarity, we introduce the Action-Following Index (AFI), which measures normalized reconstruction gain over a static-prefix baseline. On 40 held-out 3D volumes, SonoAtlasWorld achieves liver and macro-organ AFI of 0.428 and 0.262, compared with at most 0.117 and 0.038 for the tested action- and geometry-conditioned methods. Long-rollout alignment reduces brightness drift by 3.46x at the end of 40 repeated scan cycles. SonoAtlasWorld also generates offline policy-training demonstrations and supports anatomically grounded closed-loop evaluation. Scaling the generated training data improves key-plane acquisition success from 48.2% to 81.2% on unseen anatomies in simulation, demonstrating its utility for robotic ultrasound policy learning.



### Effectiveness of Synthetic Defects for Anomaly Detection in Industrial Small Load Carriers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26868v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26868v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 22:31:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 22:31:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Vincent Wahyudi, Kuniko Paxton, Amila Akagić, Martin Storath, Dhavalkumar Thakker
- **Comment**: Submitted to ESANN 2027
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent industrial anomaly detection methods use procedural noise to generate synthetic training defects. Their effectiveness in real-world settings and the impact of different noise generation methods have not been sufficiently investigated. We evaluate the effectiveness of training using synthetic defects for both supervised and unsupervised methods, the impact of different noise generation methods, and the differences in detection performance between benchmark datasets and a real-world small load carrier (SLC) dataset. Trained on synthetic defects only, a supervised classifier reaches 0.65 AUROC on real SLC defects, while three unsupervised methods using the same kind of noise (Perlin) reach 0.87--0.90. Except for SuperSimpleNet, which fails with the foreground-confined fractal Brownian motion (fBm) recipe, models trained with the custom recipe or with nine other noise types do not differ reliably from those trained with Perlin noise. Finally, results with synthetic defects on benchmark datasets do not carry over to the SLC dataset, possibly because the synthetic defects differ too much from the real ones (a larger angle in feature space). These results call for caution when training models on synthetic defect images.



### Your Judge Inherits Your Prompt: In-Context Label Bias in an LLM-as-Judge Is Directional, Not Symmetric
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26881v1
- **DOI**: 10.18653/2026.blackboxnlp-1.40
- **Categories**: **cs.CL**, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26881v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 22:48:23+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 22:48:23+00:00
- **Authors**: Abdullah Ahmad, Ahmed J. O. Ahmed, Luca Citi
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 2 figures, 4 tables. Published in the Proceedings of the 9th BlackboxNLP Workshop: Analyzing and Interpreting Neural Networks for NLP (2026)
- **Journal**: Proceedings of the 9th BlackboxNLP Workshop: Analyzing and Interpreting Neural Networks for NLP, pages 696-706, 2026
- **Summary**: LLM-as-judge has become a standard way to score open-ended model outputs, yet the judge is itself a frozen language model steered by a few in-context examples, and such models are known to absorb the label balance of their demonstrations rather than treat them as neutral illustrations. We ask whether this label bias reaches an LLM judge used inside a multimodal (video question answering) evaluation pipeline, and what form it takes. Using a fully frozen generator-judge pipeline, we hold the generated answers fixed and vary only the Yes/No balance of the judge's few-shot examples across four demonstration sets. Each verdict is validated against a two-annotator human consensus on 200 examples per dataset. The judge's acceptance rate stays close to human consensus when its examples are balanced or skewed toward rejection, but jumps sharply (by about 30 points on NExT-QA and 20 on MSVD-QA, with a strongly one-sided error profile) when the examples are skewed toward acceptance; the opposite skew produces no matching drop. The bias is therefore one-directional within the tested range of label ratios. A separate five-draw analysis shows that results from a single example draw are unreliable, varying by up to 18 points, more than any difference between prompting conditions. We conclude that demonstration label bias in an LLM judge is a directional, inflation-only failure mode that can fabricate apparent benchmark gains but not losses, and that it is cheaply avoided by balancing the judge's examples and reporting results averaged over several draws.



### TouchBench: Can Vision-Language Models Feel Invisible Physical Properties?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26883v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26883v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 22:52:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 22:52:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Ziyao Zeng, Ziyi Kou, Chuan Qin, Mia Huang, Roger Boldu, Zhiwen Fan, Daniel Rakita, Eric Whitmire, Li Guan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used for embodied perception and reasoning, yet their physical understanding is largely evaluated on questions answerable from images. Many properties critical to manipulation, such as an opaque bottle's fill level or an object's compliance, may only be revealed through touch. We introduce TouchBench, an egocentric vision-language-tactile benchmark that distinguishes visually answerable questions (Tier A) from those involving properties hidden from the camera (Tier B). It pairs head-mounted video with synchronized 137-channel whole-hand tactile sensing, comprising 900 clips from five participants across 11 tasks, with 900 absolute and 4,102 relative questions. Four state-of-the-art VLMs average 66.4% accuracy on Tier A relative questions but only 49.8% on Tier B, near chance (50%). To bridge this gap, we propose Tactile-as-Prompt (TaP), which presents 34 interpretable tactile statistics as text, enabling VLMs to reason with touch without a learned tactile encoder. TaP raises zero-shot Tier B relative accuracy to 62.9% and reaches 73.1% after leave-one-user-out fine-tuning, outperforming video alone (67.4%) and a learned tactile-token encoder (67.3%). The gains extend to unseen objects and users. On a separate 20-hour in-the-wild corpus, tactile text improves fine-tuned contact-question accuracy from 67.1% to 75.5%. These results highlight the limits of vision-only physical reasoning and establish language as an effective interface for tactile perception.



### Perceive Once, Escalate on Demand: Handing Off Visual KV Memory from Small to Large VLMs
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26902v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26902v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 23:32:52+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 23:32:52+00:00
- **Authors**: Qiang Zhou, Jianing Deng, Siyan Dong, Jingxian Chen, Han-Yi Lin, Jingtong Hu
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 6 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: An embodied agent cannot afford a large vision-language model (VLM) at every step of a long video, so a cascade runs a small VLM continuously and escalates only uncertain decisions to a large one. Each escalation, however, forces the large model to re-encode a history the small model has already perceived, and this dominates its cost. We present Baton, which hands the large model the small model's visual memory, its key-value (KV) cache over the history. Baton rests on one finding: the large model needs a usable memory, not a faithful copy of its own. A map trained to reproduce the large model's cache matches it most closely, yet brings no gain over the small model alone. Baton instead distills a per-layer linear map, without labels, from the large model's own answers, with the large model encoding only the current frame. Interventions on the map trace the gain to one direction, carried by the middle third of the large model's layers. On RoboMME, Baton beats the small model alone, a pruned or shortened re-watch, and a text memory; as the escalation channel of a cascade it matches re-watching to within about one point at 28-47% less compute, and 8x less per escalation at 64 frames. Its gain grows with the large model, from 8B to 32B, tracks the large model's headroom across eight model pairs from four families, and transfers zero-shot to OpenEQA.



### What Does a Zoom Actually Add? Decomposing Fine-Grained Perception Failures in Vision-Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26904v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.CL
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26904v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 23:38:17+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 23:38:17+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhixuan Ge, Anqi Li, Kexin Geng, Wei Qiu
- **Comment**: 27 pages, 17 figures, 19 tables. Code and per-sample outputs: https://github.com/gezhixuan/zoomdecomp
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Zooming into a region often turns a wrong answer from a vision-language model (VLM) into a right one, and a growing literature takes this as evidence that the model lacked the pixels. But a zoom changes several things at once. It removes the surrounding context, re-tokenizes, at a higher density, pixels the model has already seen, and, if the first pass was downsampled, exposes detail the model never received. We separate these factors for each failure. Holding the first pass fixed, we vary only the second look and assign every failure to the cheapest one that fixes it: reusing cached tokens, isolating the region, re-tokenizing the seen pixels, or acquiring new ones. The study covers 2,753 zoom-rescued failures of Qwen2.5-VL, Qwen3-VL and InternVL3 on V*, ZoomBench and TextVQA, at first-pass budgets from 17x fewer pixels than the source to native resolution. First, most rescued failures did not lack the pixels: upsampling the pixels the model had already seen fixes 47-73% of them even at 17x downsampling, against 1-3% for a random region. Second, the first-pass budget decides how often new pixels are needed: depending on the benchmark, new pixels are required for 23-50% of the rescues at 17x and for 12-23% at 4x. The missing-pixel reading and the accounts that dispute it thus hold at different budgets. Third, the useful magnification is large and differs between objects and text: seen pixels fix more failures up to 6-12x their first-pass size per side on objects and saturate by 4x on text, and even native pixels gain from being rendered beyond native resolution. Allowing any magnification, 68-94% of the failures at 17x need no new pixels. These models more often need a better rendering of evidence they already have than new pixels, which motivates re-tokenizing a region before acquiring it.



### A High-Compression Transformer VAE for Efficient and Controllable 3D Medical Image Generation with Diffusion Transformers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26907v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **eess.IV**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26907v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-19 23:44:12+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 23:44:12+00:00
- **Authors**: Bo Liu, Bingliang Zhang, Yubo Wang, Yunxiang Li, Yang Yang, Janine M. Lupo, Hui Lin
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Many medical image processing tasks, such as completing missing regions or correcting acquisition artifacts, can be cast as volumetric generation conditioned on observations of the same patient. Diffusion Transformers offer scalable capacity and flexible conditioning for this setting, but they operate on a learned image representation that must preserve fine anatomical and pathological detail while keeping complete three-dimensional volumes tractable. We bring Transformer variational autoencoding and multimodal diffusion together to address this challenge. Trained on BrainTumor-256K, a 249M Transformer VAE encodes and decodes $256^3$ volumes 7.6x faster than a comparably sized CNN trained on the same collection. Their PSNR difference is near zero with training-sized crop inputs, but the Transformer leads by 0.749 dB with full-volume inputs and an unchanged scoring region. Increasing Transformer capacity to 499M improves PSNR across all four brain MRI evaluation sets while retaining 3.8x lower encoding-decoding latency than the CNN. Fast encoding also allows conditions to be constructed dynamically in image space during latent diffusion training. Within the compressed representation, a shared Diffusion Transformer integrates text and spatial references for volumetric synthesis, completion and restoration. The brain-trained autoencoder further transfers to non-brain MRI and CT without target-domain fine-tuning, extending its use beyond the anatomy and modality on which it was learned. The framework provides a reusable representation and conditioning interface for efficient, controllable medical image generation. Code is available at https://github.com/OncoMMAI/brainmri-vit-vae3d.
